          +|\mathcal L_ed_{\ell,e}|\bigr)\biggr\}.           \label{eq:moving-core-voltage-defect-recursion}
\end{align}
Equations \cref{eq:moving-core-formal-defect-jets,%
eq:moving-core-resource-defect-recursion} give the identical bound for the
new \(d_{j,e}\)-terms.  Thus, for each fixed \(j\le h\),
\begin{equation}
 M_j\le C_h\left\{\varepsilon+
                  \sum_{0\le\ell<j}M_\ell\right\}.           \label{eq:moving-core-triangular-induction}
\end{equation}
The base row is the pair of exact identities just proved.  Finite induction
in \(j\) establishes \cref{eq:moving-core-jet-bound}.  Applying the Hermite
bound \cref{eq:collar-Hermite-bound} first to \(b_e\) and then to
\(\mathcal L_eb_e\) proves \cref{eq:moving-core-cap-bound}.
All recursions are finite smooth algebraic expressions on a common
same-sign coefficient collar; hence their explicit formulas also justify
the \(e\)-differentiability used above, uniformly on that collar.

It remains to check the seam and the sharpness claim.  The current value of
both voltage histories is \(V_{0,N}(e)\), so
\(\gamma_e^{\rm ph}(0)=0\).  Differentiating the first identity in
\cref{eq:moving-core-first-defects} along \(\mathcal L_e\) gives
\begin{equation*}
 \delta\partial_\theta\gamma_e^{\rm ph}(0)
 =\delta\mathcal L_eb_{1,e}=-q_0(e)R_{z,N}'(e).
\end{equation*}
Let \(\varpi_e^{\rm rc}\) denote the endpoint resource jet germ produced by
the raw compatibility recursion and put
\(\varpi_e^0(\theta)=w_{\rm seed,N}(r_e^0(\theta))\).  At the level of
endpoint jets define the normal resource-defect phase column by
\begin{equation*}
 p_e^{\rm ph}=-\mathcal L_e(\varpi_e^{\rm rc}-\varpi_e^0).
\end{equation*}
The two germs have the same current value.  Applying
\(-\mathcal L_e\) to the second identity in
\cref{eq:moving-core-first-defects} therefore gives
\begin{equation*}
 p_e^{\rm ph}(0)=0,
 \qquad
 \delta\partial_\theta p_e^{\rm ph}(0)
 =\delta^2q_0(e)R_{q,N}'(e)
 =\varepsilon q_0(e)R_{q,N}'(e).
\end{equation*}
These are \cref{eq:moving-core-seam-match} and agree with the two rows of
\cref{eq:constant-phase-residual-column}.

For the final assertion, take the displayed special case.  Then
\(R_{z,N}=\varepsilon q_0z_{0,N}'\), so
\begin{equation*}
 \delta\mathcal L_eb_{1,e}
 =-\delta^2q_0(e)\partial_e\{q_0(e)z_{0,N}'(e)\}.
\end{equation*}
Use \(z_{0,N}'(e)=2g_Ne+O(e^2)\), let
\(e=-\rho_\delta\to0\), and use the reduced-speed bounds.  This gives
\cref{eq:moving-core-sharpness}.  Since \(\varepsilon=\delta^2\), the
normalized collar column can therefore remain of order one.  None of this
constructs a compatible resource extension on the whole old-history
interval, so no past-orbit claim has entered the proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of
\cref{lem:raw-compatible-base-collar-refinement}]
Fix \(e\).  Since
\[
 \frac{\delta}{|e|}
 \le\frac{2\alpha}{S_\delta}=o(1),
\]
after decreasing \(\delta_0\) choose nested same-sign annuli
\[
 e\in\operatorname{int}I_e,\qquad
 I_e\Subset J_{0,e}\Subset J_e\Subset
       \{0<|r|<r_0\},
\]
with \(|r|\asymp|e|\) on \(J_e\), uniformly in \(e\), and such that
\[
 \left\{\Phi_{q_0}^{-\tau}(r):
      r\in\overline I_e,\ 0\le\tau\le\delta\theta_m\right\}
 \subset J_{0,e}.
\]
In the weighted norm of \cref{eq:backtrack-weighted-norm}, the critical
voltage \(f_N(r)=r\mathbf1+z_{0,N}(r)\) obeys
\begin{equation}
 \|f_N\|_{h+2,|e|;J_e}
 +\|q_0z_{0,N}'\|_{h,|e|;J_e}
 \le C_h|e|.                                      \label{eq:base-collar-critical-size}
\end{equation}
The zero-delay summand of \(\mathcal D_{0,N}\) vanishes as a complete
difference.  Applying \cref{prop:physical-backtrack-calculus} with
\(I=I_e\), \(J_0=J_{0,e}\), and \(J=J_e\) at order \(h+1\), and retaining
the \(\delta/|e|\) factor in each composition difference, gives
\begin{equation}
 \|\mathcal D_{0,N}\|_{h+1,|e|;I_e}\le C_h\delta. \label{eq:base-collar-delay-size}
\end{equation}
For the collective residual, write
\[
 w_{0,N}'(r)=r\,m_N(r).
\]
The critical expansion and high-regularity bounds imply, after decreasing
\(r_{\rm out}\),
\[
 0<c_m\le |m_N(r)|\le C_m,\qquad
 \|m_N\|_{C^{h+1}(J_e)}\le C_h .
\]
The exact reduced-speed quotient is therefore
\[
 q_0(r)
 =\frac{r-\varepsilon\nu}{w_{0,N}'(r)}
 =\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon\nu}{r}\right)m_N(r)^{-1}.
\]
Hence
\[
 \max_{0\le j\le h-1}|e|^j
       |\partial_r^{j+1}q_0(r)|
 \le C_h\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{|e|^2}\right)
 \le C_h,\qquad r\in I_e.
\]
On the other hand, \cref{eq:base-collar-delay-size} gives
\[
 |e|^j|\partial_r^{j+1}\mathcal D_{0,c,N}(r)|
 \le C_h\frac{\delta}{|e|}
 \le\frac{C_h}{S_\delta},
 \qquad 0\le j\le h-1.
\]
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
 \max_{0\le j\le h-1}|e|^j
 \left|\partial_r^j
   (\mathcal D_{0,c,N}-q_0)'(r)\right|
 \le C_h,\qquad r\in I_e.                         \label{eq:base-collar-collective-derivative-size}
\end{equation}
The definitions in \cref{eq:nonlinear-tracker-data} therefore give
\begin{align}
 \|R_{z,N}\|_{h,|e|;I_e}
 &\le C_h\varepsilon(|e|+\delta),\notag\\
 \|R_{q,N}\|_{h-1,|e|;I_e}
 &\le C_h\varepsilon.                             \label{eq:base-collar-residual-size}
\end{align}

Along the formal moving core, each \(\theta\)-derivative is
\(\delta q_0(r)\partial_r\).  The weighted product calculus and
\cref{eq:base-collar-residual-size} therefore imply, for
\(0\le m\le h-1\),
\begin{align}
 \delta^{m+1}
 \|\partial_\theta^mE_v^0(0)\|_N
 &\le C_h\varepsilon(|e|+\delta)
       \left(\frac{\delta^2}{|e|}\right)^m,\notag\\
 \delta^{m+1}|\partial_\theta^mE_w^0(0)|
 &\le C_h\varepsilon\delta^2
       \left(\frac{\delta^2}{|e|}\right)^m,       \label{eq:base-collar-formal-defect-jets}
\end{align}
where \(E_v^0,E_w^0\) are defined in
\cref{eq:moving-core-formal-defects}.  Since
\(|e|\ge\rho_\delta=\delta S_\delta/(2\alpha)\), the factor
\(\delta^2/|e|\) is bounded and tends to zero uniformly.

Repeat the triangular compatibility recursion in the proof of
\cref{lem:raw-compatible-moving-core-collar}, but do not apply
\(\mathcal L_e\).  Equations
\cref{eq:moving-core-resource-defect-recursion,%
eq:base-collar-formal-defect-jets} give, successively for
\(1\le j\le h\),
\begin{align}
 \delta^j\|b_{j,e}\|_N
 &\le C_h\left\{\varepsilon(|e|+\delta)
   +\sum_{0\le\ell<j}\delta^\ell
       (\|b_{\ell,e}\|_N+|d_{\ell,e}|)\right\},\notag\\
 \delta^j|d_{j,e}|
 &\le C_h\left\{\varepsilon(|e|+\delta)
   +\sum_{0\le\ell<j}\delta^\ell
       (\|b_{\ell,e}\|_N+|d_{\ell,e}|)\right\}.   \label{eq:base-collar-refined-recursion}
\end{align}
The base values vanish, and the first rows are the exact identities in
\cref{eq:moving-core-first-defects}.  Finite induction proves the first
inequality in \cref{eq:base-collar-vanishing-jets}.  The second follows
from \(\delta\le C\rho_\delta\le C|e|\).  Applying the Hermite estimate
\cref{eq:collar-Hermite-bound} proves
\cref{eq:base-collar-vanishing-cap}; substitution of the definition of
\(\rho_\delta\) proves \cref{eq:base-collar-inner-scale}.

Finally, in the special case of
\cref{eq:moving-core-sharpness},
\(\nu=0\), \(\mathcal D_{0,\perp,N}=0\), and
\(z_{0,N}(r)=g_Nr^2+O(r^3)\), one has
\[
 R_{z,N}(e)
 =2\varepsilon q_0(e)e\,g_N+O(\varepsilon e^2).
\]
Thus, for any fixed admissible example with \(g_N\ne0\), or uniformly
for a family satisfying
\(\inf_N\|g_N\|_N>0\),
\[
 \delta\|b_{1,e}\|_N=\|R_{z,N}(e)\|_N
 \ge c\varepsilon|e|
\]
at the inner scale for sufficiently small \(\delta\).  Hence the
\(O(\varepsilon|e|)\) base scale cannot in general be improved.
Equation \cref{eq:moving-core-sharpness} separately shows that applying
\(\mathcal L_e\) removes this factor \(|e|\) and yields the sharp
\(O(\varepsilon)\) moving-column scale.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{prop:delayed-phase-event-core}]
Let
\[
 \mathcal P_\sigma(a,\widehat\tau)
   =(\mathscr P_{c,\sigma,N}a,0,0).
\]
Then
\(
 \mathscr B_{\phi,\sigma}^{\rm del}
 =\mathscr B_{\phi,\sigma}-\mathcal P_\sigma
\).
By \cref{eq:phase-delay-action-bound,eq:phase-event-inverse-bound},
\begin{equation*}
 \|\mathscr B_{\phi,\sigma}^{-1}\mathcal P_\sigma\|
 \le C\delta\left\{1+
          \log\frac{r_{\rm out}}{\rho_\delta}\right\}.
\end{equation*}
Because \(\rho_\delta\asymp\delta S_\delta\), the right-hand side tends
to zero.  After decreasing \(\delta_0\), the Neumann series for
\(
 (I-\mathscr B_{\phi,\sigma}^{-1}\mathcal P_\sigma)^{-1}
\)
converges and gives
\[
 (\mathscr B_{\phi,\sigma}^{\rm del})^{-1}
 =(I-\mathscr B_{\phi,\sigma}^{-1}\mathcal P_\sigma)^{-1}
       \mathscr B_{\phi,\sigma}^{-1}.
\]
Its geometric-series estimate is
\cref{eq:delayed-phase-event-inverse-bound}.  Constants lie in the
bordered phase variable and are annihilated by \(\mathscr P_{\sigma,N}\)
by \cref{eq:constant-phase-delay-annihilation}; no delay-generation
argument was used.  This also proves all stated scope boundaries.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{prop:event-aligned-normal-traces}]
The chain rule at a moving terminal time gives
\[
 \left.\frac{\dd}{\dd\lambda}\right|_0
 p_\lambda(S_\lambda)
 =\dot p(S)+p_*'(S)\dot S.
\]
Applying the same rule pointwise to the history trace gives
\[
 \left.\frac{\dd}{\dd\lambda}\right|_0
 Z_\lambda(S_\lambda-L+\theta)
 =\dot Z(S-L+\theta)+Z_*'(S-L+\theta)\dot S.
\]
Substitution of
\(\dot S=\delta^{-1}\widehat\tau\) proves
\cref{eq:raw-moving-normal-traces}.  Differentiating the event in
\cref{eq:event-aligned-section-row} gives
\[
 \xi(S)+r_*'(S)\dot S
 =\xi(S)+q_e\widehat\tau=b_{\rm raw}.
\]
Now write
\[
 \dot p(S)
 =n_p(S)+\frac{p_*'(S)}{\delta q_e}\xi(S)
\]
and use the analogous identity for \(\dot Z(S-L+\theta)\).  The two
terms containing \(\xi(S)\) and \(\widehat\tau\) combine into
\[
 \frac{x_*'}{\delta q_e}
       \{\xi(S)+q_e\widehat\tau\}
 =\frac{x_*'}{\delta q_e}b_{\rm raw},
\]
for \(x_*=p_*\) or the translated \(Z_*\)-history.  This proves
\cref{eq:event-aligned-triangular-rows}.  If \(b_{\rm raw}=0\), no
\(\widehat\tau\)-column remains.  At \(Z_*=p_*=0\), both normal time
derivatives in \cref{eq:raw-moving-normal-traces} vanish, proving the
reduced-base statement.

For a fixed regular handoff section, the implicit-function theorem and
\(r_*'(s_h)\ne0\) give
\[
 \dot s_h=-\frac{\xi(s_h)}{r_*'(s_h)}.
\]
The chain rule for
\(Z_\lambda(s_h(\lambda)+\theta)\) and
\(p_\lambda(s_h(\lambda))\) now gives
\cref{eq:fixed-r-handoff-traces}.  The formula contains neither
\(S_\lambda\) nor \(\widehat\tau\).  On the reduced branch, autonomy and
the terminal condition \(r^\#(0)=e_1\) uniquely give
\cref{eq:terminal-relative-reduced-path}; its fixed-\(r\) crossing at
\(e_h=\Phi_{q_0}^{-\delta L_b}(e_1)\) is
\(\varsigma=-L_b\).  Simultaneous translation of the component equations
carries their Green kernels to translated kernels with the same time
differences and identical bounds.  Therefore the fixed reduced-base
component operator has the same norm estimates in these coordinates,
without differentiating a moving trace.  For a general
moving nonlinear family, \(s_h(\lambda)-S_\lambda\) need not remain fixed;
the proof makes no flushing claim for that family.

Finally, direct differentiation of
\cref{eq:moving-trace-strong-norm} gives
\[
 \|\delta^{-1}x'\|_{d,\delta}
 =\max_{0\le j\le d}\delta^{j-1}
       \|\partial_s^{j+1}x\|_\infty
 \le\delta^{-2}\|x\|_{d+1,\delta}.
\]
The loss is sharp.  For a fixed nonzero smooth compactly supported \(f\),
put
\[
 x_\delta(s)=\delta^2f((s-S)/\delta).
\]
Then
\(\|x_\delta\|_{d+1,\delta}=O(\delta^2)\), whereas
\(\|\delta^{-1}x_\delta'\|_{d,\delta}\asymp1\).
Moreover, the derivative of the translation action is its unbounded
generator \(x\mapsto x'\).  A continuous piecewise
\(W^{d,\infty}\) function with a jump in its \(d\)-th derivative supplies
the standard failure of same-space differentiability.  This proves
\cref{eq:moving-trace-one-order-loss} and the asserted regularity boundary.
All remaining exclusions are immediate because only traces, not a solution
map or a tracker, were constructed.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of
\cref{lem:terminal-relative-trace-scale-chart}]
For a Banach space \(X\), the translation--restriction map
\[
 \mathcal T(x,t)(\varsigma)=x(t+\varsigma)
\]
is \(C^2\) from
\(C^{d+2}(K^\ell;X)\times(-\ell/2,\ell/2)\) to
\(C^d(K;X)\).  Its first two derivatives are
\begin{align}
 D\mathcal T(x,t)[h,b]
 &=\mathcal T(h,t)+b\,\mathcal T(x',t),\notag\\
 D^2\mathcal T(x,t)[(h,b),(k,c)]
 &=b\,\mathcal T(k',t)+c\,\mathcal T(h',t)
       +bc\,\mathcal T(x'',t).                   \label{eq:proof-trace-scale-translation}
\end{align}
Taylor's formula with integral remainder, applied after
\(\partial_\varsigma^j\) for \(0\le j\le d\), proves the asserted
Fr\'echet differentiability and continuity of these derivatives.  The two
unused derivatives are essential; translation on the piecewise
\(W^{d,\infty}\) space itself need not be differentiable.

By \cref{eq:trace-scale-reference-event}, the scalar inverse-function
theorem gives \(\chi_*\in C^{d+2}\) after decreasing its neighborhood.
Therefore evaluation, composition with \(\chi_*\), the translations in
\cref{eq:trace-scale-chart}, and subtraction are \(C^2\) from the strong
domain to the displayed weak target.  Formula
\cref{eq:trace-scale-reconstruction} follows directly from the definitions,
so the retained moving window loses no algebraic information.  Retaining
\(\widehat\tau\) as the first output distinguishes simultaneous translations;
the event row is then the derived functional
\(E=r^\#(0)-e_1\).  The map does not give a same-space inverse because two
derivatives were deliberately spent, and it does not retain data outside the
moving window.

We compute the jets at \((Y_*,0)\).  Since
\(t_{\widehat\tau}=\widehat\tau/\delta\),
\cref{eq:proof-trace-scale-translation} gives
\[
 DE[H_i]=\xi_i(0)+\delta^{-1}r_*'(0)\sigma_i=B_i
\]
and the two raw formulas in \cref{eq:trace-scale-raw-first}.  Moreover,
\[
 D\alpha[H_i]
 =\chi_*'(e_1)B_i
 =\frac{B_i}{r_*'(0)}
 =A_i.
\]
Differentiating the two subtractions in
\cref{eq:trace-scale-chart} gives
\[
 DN_Z^\#[H_i]
 =\mathsf z_i+\delta^{-1}Z_*'\sigma_i-Z_*'A_i
 =\mathsf z_i-\frac{Z_*'}{\delta q_e}\xi_i(0),
\]
and the same calculation with \(p_*\) gives the other line of
\cref{eq:trace-scale-first-triangularization}.  Adding
\(Z_*'DE/(\delta q_e)\), or its \(p_*\) counterpart, recovers the raw
trace and proves the two right-hand identities there.

The second derivative of the terminal scalar evaluation is exactly
\[
 D^2\{r(t_{\widehat\tau})\}[H_1,H_2]=R_{12}.
\]
For the inverse function,
\[
 \chi_*'(e_1)=\frac1{r_*'(0)},\qquad
 \chi_*''(e_1)=-\frac{r_*''(0)}{r_*'(0)^3}.
\]
Consequently
\[
 D^2\alpha[H_1,H_2]
 =\frac{R_{12}}{r_*'(0)}
   -\frac{r_*''(0)}{r_*'(0)}A_1A_2
 =A_{12}.
\]
Apply \cref{eq:proof-trace-scale-translation} to the raw term and the
ordinary second-order chain rule to
\(Z_*(\alpha+\varsigma)\).  Their difference is
\cref{eq:trace-scale-Z-second}.  Replacing \(Z_*\) and
\(\mathsf z_i\) by \(p_*\) and \(\mathsf p_i\) proves
\cref{eq:trace-scale-p-second}.  If both directions contain only
\(\sigma_i\), then \(A_i=\sigma_i/\delta\) and \(A_{12}=0\); both
event-aligned derivatives vanish, which proves the stated pure-column
cancellation.

Finally,
\[
 D_{\widehat\tau}\mathcal T x
 =\delta^{-1}\mathcal T x',\qquad
 D_{\widehat\tau}^2\mathcal T x
 =\delta^{-2}\mathcal T x''.
\]
The definition of \(\|\cdot\|_{d,\delta}\) gives
\[
 \|\delta^{-1}x'\|_{d,\delta}
 \le\delta^{-2}\|x\|_{d+1,\delta},\qquad
 \|\delta^{-2}x''\|_{d,\delta}
 \le\delta^{-4}\|x\|_{d+2,\delta},
\]
which is \cref{eq:trace-scale-translation-losses}.  The scope exclusions
follow because no equation for \(Y\), and no solution operator into the
strong terminal space, entered the construction.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of
\cref{lem:endpoint-centered-duration-selector}]
The current interval, its delay enlargement, and the entry history chart
are fixed.  Hence pure \(\tau\)-differentiation annihilates
\(R_\gamma\), \(R_{\rm RFDE}\), the seam and causal-entry rows, and the
entry evaluation.  The moving-evaluation chain rule gives
\begin{align*}
 D E(Y_*,0)[v,\zeta]
 &=\frac{\xi(S_*)}{q_{0,e}}
   +\frac{q_{*,e}}{q_{0,e}}\zeta,\\
 D\{p(S(\tau))\}(Y_*,0)[v,\zeta]
 &=\mathsf p(S_*)+\delta^{-1}p_*'(S_*)\zeta,\\
 D\{Z(S(\tau)-L+\cdot)\}(Y_*,0)[v,\zeta]
 &=\mathsf z(S_*-L+\cdot)
   +\delta^{-1}Z_*'(S_*-L+\cdot)\zeta.
\end{align*}
This proves
\cref{eq:endpoint-selector-raw-border,eq:endpoint-selector-raw-terminal-column}.
The fixed entry normal row has no selector dependence.

In the terminal scalar slot,
\begin{equation*}
 K_pc_{\rm ter}
 =\frac{q_{0,e}p_*'(S_*)}{\delta q_{*,e}}
       \frac{q_{*,e}}{q_{0,e}}
 =\frac{p_*'(S_*)}{\delta}.
\end{equation*}
The same calculation in the terminal history space gives
\(K_{Z,L}c_{\rm ter}=\delta^{-1}Z_*'(S_*-L+\cdot)\).
Thus \(C_{\rm ter}^{\rm raw}=Kc_{\rm ter}\).  The upper row after
\cref{eq:endpoint-selector-nonlinear-row-shear} is
\begin{equation*}
 (L^{\rm raw}-K\ell_{\rm ter})v
 +(C_{\rm ter}^{\rm raw}-Kc_{\rm ter})\zeta
 =L^{\rm ea}v,
\end{equation*}
while the event row is unchanged.  This proves
\cref{eq:endpoint-selector-event-aligned-border}.  Its terminal scalar
component is
\begin{equation*}
 \mathsf p(S_*)
 -\frac{p_*'(S_*)}{\delta q_{*,e}}\xi(S_*),
\end{equation*}
and its terminal history component is
\begin{equation*}
 \mathsf z(S_*-L+\cdot)
 -\frac{Z_*'(S_*-L+\cdot)}{\delta q_{*,e}}\xi(S_*).
\end{equation*}
These are the event-aligned expressions in the cited trace formulas.  The
row shear sends raw data \((f^{\rm raw},b)\) to
\((f^{\rm raw}-Kb,b)\); forward substitution gives
\cref{eq:endpoint-selector-triangular-inverse}.

Under \(v=w+A\tau\), direct collection of the \(\tau\)-terms gives
\cref{eq:endpoint-selector-domain-shear}; substituting those two columns
in the Schur expression proves
\cref{eq:endpoint-selector-domain-schur-invariance}.  For the upper range
shear, put \(\alpha=\ell L^{-1}K\).  The rank-one resolvent identity gives
\begin{equation*}
 \ell(L-K\ell)^{-1}
 =\frac{\ell L^{-1}}{1-\alpha}.
\end{equation*}
Therefore
\begin{equation*}
 c-\ell(L-K\ell)^{-1}(C-Kc)
 =c-\frac{\ell L^{-1}C-\alpha c}{1-\alpha}
 =\frac{c-\ell L^{-1}C}{1-\alpha},
\end{equation*}
which is \cref{eq:endpoint-selector-range-schur-law}.  Finally,
\cref{eq:endpoint-selector-structured-germ} identifies the endpoint speed,
so \cref{eq:endpoint-selector-scalar-margin} is exactly
\cref{eq:reference-pulled-terminal-coefficient}.  The proof used only the
supplied fixed envelope and bounded terminal traces; it supplied no hybrid
upper inverse or nonlinear endpoint family.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of
\cref{prop:endpoint-normal-shear-identity}]
The resource calculation in the proof of
\cref{prop:fold-time-tracker-normal-form} gives, before multiplication by
the extra factor of \(\delta\), the exact identity
\begin{equation}
 p_s=\delta w_{0,N}'(r)Q
       +\delta\varepsilon q
          (\mathcal D_{0,c,N}-q_0)'(r).          \label{eq:proof-endpoint-resource-speed}
\end{equation}
Evaluate it at \(s=S_*\), where \(r=e\) and \(q=q_{*,e}\), and use
\(
 R_{q,N}=\varepsilon q_0
       (\mathcal D_{0,c,N}-q_0)'
\).  Substitution in the definition of the selector column gives
\begin{align*}
 K_p
 &=\frac{q_0(e)p_*'(S_*)}{\delta q_{*,e}}\\
 &=\frac{q_0(e)}{q_{*,e}}w_{0,N}'(e)Q_e
   +\varepsilon q_0(e)
       (\mathcal D_{0,c,N}-q_0)'(e)\\
 &=\frac{q_0(e)}{q_{*,e}}w_{0,N}'(e)Q_e+R_{q,N}(e),
\end{align*}
which proves \cref{eq:endpoint-normal-shear-identity}.  The coefficient
bounds in the stated endpoint annulus, together with
\(|R_{q,N}(e)|\le C\varepsilon\), now give the first line of
\cref{eq:endpoint-normal-shear-scale-bound}.  Multiplication by
\(\rho_\delta/(\varepsilon R_\delta)\) gives its second line and the two
displayed sufficient conditions.

It remains to identify the local anchor.  In the raw-compatible collar,
the current voltage agrees with the formal moving core and the Hermite
correction is supported away from all strict delay reads.  Moreover,
\cref{eq:moving-core-first-defects} gives
\(
 b_{1,e}=-\delta^{-1}R_{z,N}(e)\in E_N
\), hence \(\pi_N^Tb_{1,e}=0\).  Thus its collective speed at the current
endpoint is the formal speed:
\begin{equation*}
 q_{*,e}=q_0(e),\qquad Q_e=0.
\end{equation*}
Moreover, for the resource defect
\(p=\varpi_e^0-\varpi_e^{\rm rc}\),
\cref{eq:moving-core-first-defects} gives
\begin{equation*}
 p_*'(0)=c_{1,e}^0-c_{1,e}^{\rm rc}
        =\delta R_{q,N}(e).
\end{equation*}
Hence \(K_p^{\rm rc}=R_{q,N}(e)\).  The uniform residual-jet bound gives
\(|R_{q,N}(e)|\le C\varepsilon\), and therefore
\(
 \kappa_{p,\delta}^{\rm rc}
 \le C\rho_\delta/R_\delta=o(1)
\)
by \cref{eq:proof-diagonal-raw-scale-ratios}.  No forward continuation
of this local collar was used, which proves the final scope statement.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of
\cref{prop:repelling-speed-aligned-terminal-row}]
The algebraic row in \cref{eq:fixed-phase-normal-block}, evaluated at

\(
s=S_r
\), is
\begin{equation*}
 \varepsilon Q_y(e_r)
 =p_y(S_r)-\ell_N(e_r)Z_y(S_r)
   +\varepsilon\mathscr D_{c,N}^{\rm lin}
       [\gamma_y,Z_y](S_r).
\end{equation*}
This proves \cref{eq:speed-aligned-linear-speed}.  The terminal point lies
past every strict delay, and the graph norm controls
\(p_y(S_r)/(\varepsilon R_\delta)\), the terminal voltage trace, and all
current delay differences.  On the terminal annulus
\(
|\ell_N(e_r)|\le CR_\delta
\), while the fixed-delay coefficients in
\(\mathscr D_{c,N}^{\rm lin}\) are uniformly bounded.  The same
algebraic-row estimate used in
\cref{eq:normal-phase-pointwise-Q} therefore proves
\cref{eq:speed-aligned-row-bound}.

It remains to calculate the new row on the old terminal scalar column.
Write
\(
(Z^{\rm ter},p^{\rm ter})
\)
for the current part of
\(
\mathfrak u_r^{\rm ter}1
\).
The current-loop estimate in the proof of
\cref{prop:diagonal-terminal-component-green} gives, before strict-delay
generations are inserted,
\begin{equation}
 \|Z^{\rm ter}\|_{C^0([S_r-\theta_m,S_r])}
 \le CR_\delta .                              \label{eq:proof-speed-row-current-column}
\end{equation}
Indeed, the terminal scalar datum has size
\(
m_{r,T}^{-1}
\)
in the local scalar norm and the scalar-to-voltage current-loop kernel has
size
\(
O(R_\delta m_{r,T})
\).
Equivalently, in the unscaled
\(\|Z\|+|p|/R_\delta\) state norm the datum \(p(S_r)=1\) has size
\(R_\delta^{-1}\), while the terminal scalar-to-voltage block has norm
\(O(R_\delta^2)\); their product is the \(O(R_\delta)\) bound above.
Each strict-delay generation costs
\(
O(\varepsilon/m_{r,T})=O(\varepsilon/R_\delta)
\).  After the speed row is applied, the geometric sum of the generated
terms is therefore bounded by \(C\varepsilon/R_\delta\).  The compatible
entry-feedback column has bounded normalized amplitude and its final row
trace is flushed by
\(
\mathcal E_{r,T}(\delta)
\).
Summing the component series and using
\(
p^{\rm ter}(S_r)=1
\)
therefore gives
\begin{equation}
 \left|
 -\ell_N(e_r)Z^{\rm ter}(S_r)
 +\varepsilon\mathscr D_{c,N}^{\rm lin}
     [\gamma^{\rm ter},Z^{\rm ter}](S_r)
 \right|
 \le C\left\{R_\delta^2+\frac{\varepsilon}{R_\delta}
          +\mathcal E_{r,T}(\delta)\right\}.     \label{eq:proof-speed-row-column-error}
\end{equation}
This is exactly
\cref{eq:speed-aligned-row-margin}.  Since every term in
\(
\omega_{r,\delta}^{\rm sp}
\)
tends to zero, \(|\chi_{r,\delta}|\ge1/2\) after decreasing
\(\delta_0\).

For prescribed distributed data and old history, first solve with zero old
future scalar row, obtaining
\(
y_0=G_r(\gamma_0,0,F,H)
\).
Adding a multiple of
\(
\mathfrak u_r^{\rm ter}
\)
does not change those data, and the coefficient forced by the new scalar
row is precisely the quotient in
\cref{eq:speed-aligned-row-inverse}.  This proves the row-replacement
isomorphism and, by restriction, its source-split versions.

Finally, the nonlinear algebraic reconstruction
\cref{eq:fold-time-speed-reconstruction} gives
\(
\mathscr B_r^{\rm sp,nl}=\varepsilon Q_e
\)
without approximation.  At a zero of this row,
\(
Q_e=0
\), hence the moving speed is the reduced speed and
\(
q_{*,e}=q_0(e_r)
\).
The selector normalization in
\cref{eq:speed-aligned-selector-normalization} first gives
\(c_{\rm ter}=1\).  Substitution in
\cref{eq:endpoint-normal-shear-identity} then gives
\(
K_p=R_{q,N}(e_r)
\), and the estimates in
\cref{eq:speed-aligned-endpoint-shear}.  No nonlinear zero or terminal
voltage jet was constructed in this argument.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of
\cref{thm:speed-bordered-affine-endpoint-core}]
We first close the phase-to-speed row which is absent from a normal-only
row replacement.  On the repelling branch,
\cref{eq:speed-aligned-linear-speed,eq:speed-row-right-inverse} give
\[
 \varepsilon\{\mathfrak D_rT_r^{\rm rc}a
        +\mathscr P_{c,r,N}a\}(e_{r,1})
 =c_r^{\rm sp}[a]
 =\mathscr B_r^{\rm sp}
       \mathfrak v_r^{\rm sp}c_r^{\rm sp}[a].
\]
Subtracting the last response proves
\cref{eq:speed-projected-phase-kernel}.  Thus \(T_r^{\rm sp}a\), rather
than \(T_r^{\rm rc}a\), is the phase column in the kernel of the full
linear endpoint-speed row.

We next estimate this projection.  Put
\(A=\|a\|_{\mathsf A_{r,\mathrm{hyb}}}\).
The two phase norms and the terminal phase-column estimate give
\begin{equation}
 \|a\|_{\mathsf A_{r,\mathrm{pa}}}
 \le C\lambda_\delta^{-1}A,\qquad
 \frac{|(\mathfrak A_r^{\rm rc}a)(e_{r,1})|}{R_\delta}
 \le C\frac{\rho_\delta}{\lambda_\delta}A.       \label{eq:proof-speed-phase-endpoint-column}
\end{equation}
Directly from \(\lambda_\delta\ge S_\delta^{-2}\),
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\rho_\delta}{\lambda_\delta}
 \le\frac{\delta S_\delta^3}{2\alpha}=o(1).      \label{eq:proof-speed-phase-uniform-ratio}
\end{equation}
This estimate is uniform in the outer-scale coefficient \(\varkappa\);
introducing the weaker comparison
\(M_\delta/(2\alpha\varkappa^2)\) here would create an artificial
negative power of \(\varkappa\).
Since \(|\chi_{r,\delta}|\ge1/2\), the direct boundary-column and
terminal-trace estimates in
\cref{prop:diagonal-boundary-prefix-terminal-trace} imply
\begin{align}
 \|\mathfrak v_r^{\rm sp}c_r^{\rm sp}[a]\|_
       {\widetilde{\mathsf X}_{r,0}}
 +\|(Z_{r,T},p_{r,T})
       [\mathfrak v_r^{\rm sp}c_r^{\rm sp}[a]]\|_
       {\mathsf T_{r,T}^1([0,L_b])}
 &\le C\frac{\rho_\delta}{\lambda_\delta}A,
                                                        \label{eq:proof-speed-phase-column-state}\\
 \|\mathfrak D_r\mathfrak v_r^{\rm sp}
          c_r^{\rm sp}[a]\|_{\mathsf H_{r,\mathrm{hyb}}}
 &\le C\frac{\rho_\delta}{\lambda_\delta}A.      \label{eq:proof-speed-phase-column-action}
\end{align}
For the prefix part of the last line one uses
\cref{eq:boundary-column-global-action}; its additional factor
\(\eta_{{\rm bd},\delta}\) is at most one.  Combining these inequalities
with
\cref{eq:source-split-hybrid-phase-smallness} proves
\cref{eq:speed-projected-phase-bounds} and the first line following
\cref{eq:speed-projected-phase-small-number}.  The attracting identities
are immediate.  Since every summand in
\(\eta_{\mathrm{sp},\delta}\) tends to zero, the phase Schur operator
\[
 \mathscr S_{\sigma,\mathrm{hyb}}^{\rm sp}
 =\mathscr B_{\sigma,\mathrm{hyb}}
       -(\mathfrak A_\sigma^{\rm sp},0)
\]
is a uniform isomorphism by the same Neumann argument as before.

The speed-row replacement in
\cref{prop:repelling-speed-aligned-terminal-row} applies to each of the
three direct source summands, and the attracting normal operator is
unchanged.  Therefore
\(\mathscr N_\sigma^{\rm sp,\infty}\) is an isomorphism on the transported
domain in \cref{eq:speed-bordered-normal-domain}.  Solving first its
normal row, then the phase Schur equation, and finally the terminal event
row gives exactly
\cref{eq:affine-complete-bordered-inverse}.  Conversely those three rows
force the same formulas.  This proves the isomorphism and both uniform
operator bounds.  Notice that the last row is triangular: it determines
\(\tau\) only after \(a\) has been found.

It remains to verify the affine datum and the quantitative predictor
bounds.  The residual jets
\cref{eq:second-endpoint-residual-jets} with \(n=0\) give, throughout the
receding collars,
\begin{equation}
 \|R_{z,N}(r)\|_N\le C\varepsilon|r|,
 \qquad |R_{q,N}(r)|\le C\varepsilon.           \label{eq:proof-affine-residual-pointwise}
\end{equation}
The raw-compatible collar estimate also gives
\[
 \varepsilon^{-1}
 \|\Delta\phi_{e_{\sigma,0}}\|_{N,e_{\sigma,0},\delta}^{\sharp}
 \le C|e_{\sigma,0}|\le CR_\delta.
\]
For the terminal-free summand, its direct norm is consequently bounded by
\begin{equation}
 C\sup_{\rho_\delta\le |r|\le R_\delta}
 \left\{\frac{|r|}{\lambda_\delta}
       +\frac{\varepsilon}
              {\lambda_\delta|r|^2}\right\}
 \le C,                                         \label{eq:proof-affine-bulk-source}
\end{equation}
because \(R_\delta\le\lambda_\delta\) and
\(\varepsilon/\rho_\delta^2\le C S_\delta^{-2}
\le C\lambda_\delta\).
On the terminal annulus \(|r|\asymp r_{\sigma,T}\), and
\[
 \frac{\varepsilon}{r_{\sigma,T}m_{\sigma,T}}
 \le r_{\sigma,T}.
\]
Thus the terminal direct norm is at most \(Cr_{\sigma,T}\).
These estimates prove
\cref{eq:affine-source-summand-bounds,%
eq:affine-terminal-source-sharp-bound}.  The indicator functions only
choose the two a.e. source coordinates; they introduce neither an
approximation nor a distributional interface term.

For clarity, first apply the old-row three-summand Green operator to this
datum.  The bulk and boundary estimates give an old global state of size
\(C\lambda_\delta\).  The same holds for the special terminal datum.  On
the repelling branch this follows directly from
\cref{eq:terminal-source-state-bound}.  On the attracting branch its
sharper norm \(O(\rho_\delta)\) gives
\[
 \varepsilon^{-1}\|Z\|_N\le C\rho_\delta,\qquad
 \varepsilon^{-1}\frac{|p|}{|r|}
 \le C\frac{m_{a,T}}{\rho_\delta}\rho_\delta
 \le C\lambda_\delta
\]
on the terminal annulus, and the source is zero before that annulus.
Replacing the old repelling row by the speed row adds a terminal boundary
column.  The final-window flushing of the bulk and entry columns and
\cref{eq:terminal-source-speed-bound} show that the direct norm of this
correction is at most \(C\lambda_\delta\).  Hence
\begin{equation}
 \|y_\sigma^{\rm aff}\|_{\widetilde{\mathsf X}_{\sigma,0}}
 \le C\lambda_\delta.                           \label{eq:proof-affine-normal-state}
\end{equation}

The same summand calculation, now retaining the oriented primitives,
gives
\begin{align}
 \|\mathfrak D_\sigma y_\sigma^{\rm aff}\|_
       {\mathsf H_{\sigma,\mathrm{hyb}}}
 \le C\biggl\{&
   \lambda_\delta
   +\frac{\lambda_\delta M_\delta}{\rho_\delta}
   +R_\delta\eta_{{\rm bd},\delta}
   +R_\delta m_{\sigma,T}\biggr\}.              \label{eq:proof-affine-returned-action}
\end{align}
Indeed, the four displayed terms are respectively the pointwise ledger,
the bulk prefix obtained by integrating
\(|Q_{\rm B}|\le C\lambda_\delta m_\delta\), the boundary prefix, and the
terminal prefix.  The speed-row correction is another boundary column of
norm \(O(\lambda_\delta)\), so its pointwise and prefix terms are absorbed
by the first term.  Applying the uniform phase Schur inverse and then
\(\tau=-a(e_{\sigma,1})\) proves
\cref{eq:affine-predictor-phase-slack-bound}.

The normal terminal observations give \(C\lambda_\delta\).  The old phase
column contributes at most
\(C(\rho_\delta+M_\delta)\|a\|_{\mathsf A_{\sigma,\mathrm{hyb}}}\),
as in the proof of
\cref{eq:source-split-reconstructed-terminal-observation}, and the
speed-projection correction has the same bound by
\cref{eq:proof-speed-phase-column-state}.  This proves
\cref{eq:affine-predictor-terminal-bound}.

Finally,
\[
 \frac{\lambda_\delta M_\delta}{\rho_\delta}
 =2\alpha\varkappa^2+o(1),
\]
up to terms which vanish for fixed \(\varkappa\), while
\(\lambda_\delta\), \(R_\delta\eta_{{\rm bd},\delta}\), and
\(R_\delta m_{\sigma,T}\) tend to zero.  This proves
\cref{eq:affine-explicit-budget-limit}.  The affine speed datum is
zero, so
\(\mathfrak D_ry_r^{\rm aff}(e_{r,1})=0\); adding
\cref{eq:speed-projected-phase-kernel} proves
\cref{eq:affine-predictor-speed-row}.  Every step was linear or affine,
which proves the final scope statement.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{prop:phase-delay-action-shift}]
Put \(P=\mathscr P_{c,\sigma,N}a\).  In the algebraic row,
\begin{equation*}
 \varepsilon\widehat Q
 =\varepsilon(Q_{\rm n}+P)
 =p+\varepsilon P-\ell_NZ
      +\varepsilon\mathscr D_{c,N}^{\rm lin}[\gamma,Z],
\end{equation*}
which is the first line of
\cref{eq:shifted-phase-normal-system}.  In the \(Z\)-row, substituting
\(p=p^\sharp-\varepsilon P\) creates
\(+\varepsilon z_{0,N}'P\); this cancels the term
\(-\varepsilon z_{0,N}'P\) in
\cref{eq:exact-phase-normal-source-column}.  The remaining phase terms are
exactly \(F_\phi^\sharp\).

For the scalar row, differentiate the shift:
\(
 \delta p_s^\sharp=\delta p_s
                 +\delta\varepsilon P_s
\).
The substitution for \(p\) contributes
\(-\varepsilon w_{0,N}'P\) and
\(-\varepsilon^2\mathfrak d_NP\).  These cancel, term by term, the source
\(\varepsilon(w_{0,N}'+\varepsilon\mathfrak d_N)P\) in
\cref{eq:exact-phase-normal-source-column}.  What remains is the third
line of \cref{eq:shifted-phase-normal-system} and the structured source
\cref{eq:shifted-phase-structured-sources}.

We next differentiate the delay column only on the cells of its finite seam
partition.  Each summand of \(P\) is a bounded coefficient times
\begin{equation*}
 a(\bar r_\sigma(s))-\widehat a(s-\theta_k).
\end{equation*}
The estimate in the proof of
\cref{lem:reduced-flow-phase-defect} gives its size
\(C\delta|\bar r_\sigma(s)|^{-1}\|a\|_{\mathsf A_\phi^1}\).
On a seam cell, differentiate the coefficient and the two phase values.
The identities
\(\partial_s\bar r_\sigma=\delta q_0(\bar r_\sigma)\),
\(|r a'(r)|\le\|a\|_{\mathsf A_\phi^1}\), the weighted backtrack
calculus, and delay comparability give the same bound for \(P_s\).
The one-sided values coincide at every seam because both branches of
\(\widehat a\) take the entry value there.  Hence \(P\) is continuous and
piecewise \(W^{1,\infty}\), with no distributional seam term.  Projection
does not enlarge the bound, so the first line of
\cref{eq:shifted-phase-source-profile} follows for \(P_\perp\) as well.

Combine this estimate with
\cref{eq:phase-column-residual-jets}.  Since
\(\delta/|r|\le C/S_\delta\),
\begin{align*}
 \|F_\phi^\sharp\|_N
 &\le C\left\{\varepsilon(1+\delta/|r|)
                    +\varepsilon\delta/|r|\right\}
      \|a\|_{\mathsf A_\phi^1}
 \le C\varepsilon\|a\|_{\mathsf A_\phi^1},\\
 |H_\phi^\sharp|
 &\le C\left\{\varepsilon^2/|r|
                   +\delta\varepsilon\,\delta/|r|\right\}
      \|a\|_{\mathsf A_\phi^1}
 \le C\varepsilon^2|r|^{-1}
      \|a\|_{\mathsf A_\phi^1}.
\end{align*}
This proves \cref{eq:shifted-phase-source-profile}.

At the attracting entry, the formal constant extension in
\cref{eq:formal-phase-collar-extension} makes every phase difference zero,
so \(P(0)=0\).  The same is true at the repelling entry and leaves its
\(p(0)\)-indexed compatible feedback unchanged.  At the repelling terminal
event, \cref{prop:event-aligned-normal-traces} fixes the homogeneous normal
row \(p(S_r)=0\), which becomes
\(p^\sharp(S_r)=\varepsilon P(S_r)\).  This proves
\cref{eq:shifted-phase-boundary-rows}.

Finally, the failure of the old state norm is already present in a frozen
scalar subproblem on a unit fold-time interval.  Put
\(\rho=\rho_\delta\), take constant
\(P_\delta=\delta/\rho\), and consider
\begin{align*}
 \delta p_s&=-\rho(p+\varepsilon P_\delta),&p(0)&=0
       &&\text{(attracting)},\\
 \delta p_s&= \rho(p+\varepsilon P_\delta),&p(1)&=0
       &&\text{(repelling)}.
\end{align*}
Away from the selected boundary by \(O(\delta/\rho)\), both exact solutions
satisfy
\(
 |p|=\varepsilon|P_\delta|
       \{1+O(e^{-c\rho/\delta})\}
\).
A phase function supported on an \(r\)-window of width \(O(\delta)\), with
\(|a'|\asymp\rho^{-1}\), has bounded
\(\mathsf A_\phi^1\)-norm and produces
\(|P|\asymp\delta/\rho\) for a nonzero collective delayed layer.  Hence
\begin{equation*}
 \varepsilon^{-1}\frac{|p|}{\rho}
 \asymp\frac{\delta}{\rho^2}
 =\frac{4\alpha^2}{\delta S_\delta^2}\longrightarrow\infty.
\end{equation*}
The example obeys the same scalar sign and coefficient bounds on either
orientation.  It proves
\cref{eq:normalized-phase-response-obstruction}; the shifted speed, by
contrast, cancels in the interior of this example.  Therefore a uniform
state-space Schur estimate cannot replace the action estimate still
excluded from the proposition.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of
\cref{lem:current-absorbed-delay-splitting}]
For \(\theta_k=0\), the corresponding summand in
\cref{eq:fixed-phase-collar-delay} is the intact identity
\[
 L_{k,N}\{\widehat u_{\gamma,Z}(s)
                -\widehat u_{\gamma,Z}(s)\}=0.
\]
Delete it before splitting the delay.  For \(k\in\mathcal I_+\), write its
current part as \(L_{k,N}Z(s)\) and its shifted part as
\(-L_{k,N}\widehat Z(s-\theta_k)\).  Substitution in
\cref{eq:fixed-phase-delay-perturbation} puts every current term in
\(\mathbb A_{\rm cur}\) and every shifted term in \(\mathbb B_k\), with the
signs displayed in \cref{eq:current-absorbed-delay-blocks}.  This proves the
exact identity \cref{eq:current-absorbed-delay-equation}.

It remains to prove the attracting evolution estimate.  Let
\(\mathbb A_0\) denote the principal two-by-two matrix in
\cref{eq:fixed-phase-principal-s-system}.  In the
\(\|\cdot\|_{a,\rm out}\)-norm, the coefficient bounds and
\cref{eq:attracting-outer-buffer-range} give
\begin{align}
 \|\mathbb A_{\rm cur}-\mathbb A_0\|&\le C\varepsilon,\notag\\
 \max_{1\le i\le d}\delta^{-i}
 \|\partial_s^i\mathbb A_{\rm cur}(s)\|&\le C_d.   \label{eq:current-absorbed-coefficient-motion}
\end{align}
To justify the higher derivatives, apply
\cref{prop:physical-backtrack-calculus} to \(q_0\) and to each composition
of \(z_{0,N}\) with its exact backtrack.  The common layer bound and the
principal coefficient estimates through order \(k_d+2\) give, on the fixed
outer annulus,
\begin{equation}
 \max_{0\le j\le d+1}
 \bigl\{|\partial_r^j\mathcal D_{0,c,N}(r)|
              +|\partial_r^jq_0(r)|\bigr\}\le C_d.            \label{eq:current-absorbed-D0-jets}
\end{equation}
Consequently
\(\max_{j\le d}|\partial_r^j\mathfrak d_N|\le C_d\), and the
same calculation applied to
\(\mathscr R_{k,N}^{\rm cur},c_{k,N},b_{k,N}\) gives
\begin{equation}
 \max_{0\le j\le d}\sup_{r_{\rm out}/2\le r\le r_{\rm out}}
 \left\{\|\partial_r^j\mathbb A_{\rm cur}(r)\|
   +\varepsilon^{-1}\sum_{k\in\mathcal I_+}
        \|\partial_r^j\mathbb B_k(r)\|\right\}\le C_d.       \label{eq:current-absorbed-block-jets}
\end{equation}
Since \(\partial_s\bar r_a=\delta q_0(\bar r_a)\), the chain rule now
proves the second line of
\cref{eq:current-absorbed-coefficient-motion}.  The first line includes
\(\varepsilon\mathfrak d_N(p-\ell_NZ)\); the structural layer bound controls
the finite sum without a factor depending on \(N\).  The second line follows
from the displayed block-jet estimate.

The diagonal transverse evolution is forward stable at a fixed rate, and
the scalar coefficient satisfies \(w_{0,N}'\le-c r_{\rm out}\) on the
buffer.  The two off-diagonal maps have the same small block loop as
\cref{eq:principal-green-loop}.  The Volterra block inversion used in
the proof of \cref{prop:principal-tracker-endpoint-green} therefore gives
an evolution \(\mathbb U_{a,0}\) of the principal block with
\(\|\mathbb U_{a,0}(s,u)\|\le Ce^{-c(s-u)/\delta}\).  Duhamel's identity is
\begin{equation*}
 \mathbb U_{a,\rm cur}(s,u)=\mathbb U_{a,0}(s,u)
 +\delta^{-1}\int_u^s\mathbb U_{a,0}(s,v)
       \{\mathbb A_{\rm cur}(v)-\mathbb A_0(v)\}
       \mathbb U_{a,\rm cur}(v,u)\,\dd v.
\end{equation*}
In the exponentially weighted kernel norm the integral operator has norm
at most \(C\varepsilon\), because the \(\delta^{-1}\) prefactor is paired
with an exponential convolution of width \(O(\delta)\).  Neumann inversion
therefore gives
\[
 \|\mathbb U_{a,\rm cur}(s,u)\|
 \le Ce^{-c(s-u)/\delta}.
\]
Differentiate the evolution equations in \(s\) and \(u\).  Each derivative
of an endpoint contributes one factor \(\delta^{-1}\), while
\cref{eq:current-absorbed-coefficient-motion} controls every coefficient
commutator.  More precisely,
\(\partial_s\mathbb U=\delta^{-1}\mathbb A_{\rm cur}(s)\mathbb U\) and
\(\partial_u\mathbb U=-\delta^{-1}\mathbb U\mathbb A_{\rm cur}(u)\);
Leibniz' rule and induction on \(i+j\) prove
\cref{eq:current-absorbed-attracting-evolution}.

We now keep the slow rate visible on the diagonal family.  Write
\(R=R_\delta\).  The elementary scale ratios are
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\delta}{R}
 =\frac{\sqrt\delta}{\varkappa S_\delta^{3/2}}\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad
 \frac{\delta}{R^2}
 =\frac1{\varkappa^2S_\delta^3}\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad
 \frac{\varepsilon}{R^3}
 =\frac{\sqrt\delta}{\varkappa^3S_\delta^{9/2}}
 \longrightarrow0.                              \label{eq:proof-diagonal-buffer-ratios}
\end{equation}
On \(R/2\le r\le R\), direct differentiation of the quotient defining
\(q_0\), followed by the critical coefficient bounds, gives
\[
 |q_0'|\le C(1+\varepsilon/R^2),\qquad
 |q_0''|\le C(1+\varepsilon/R^3).
\]
Thus the order-one coefficient and exact-backtrack bounds used above
remain uniform after first fixing \(\varkappa\) and then decreasing
\(\delta\).

In the \(\|\cdot\|_{a,\delta}\)-norm, the transverse diagonal rate is
bounded below independently of \(\delta\), whereas
\(-w_{0,N}'\ge cR\).  The principal block loop is \(O(R^2)\).
To make the evolution claim independent of the earlier mixed-BVP Green
bound, let \(U_Z\) and \(u_p\) denote the two forward diagonal evolutions,
and form their causal convolution edges
\(\mathcal K_{Zp}^R\) and \(\mathcal K_{pZ}^R\) with the two principal
off-diagonal coefficients.  In a kernel norm carrying the weight
\(\exp\{cR(s-u)/(2\delta)\}\), the coefficient sizes
\[
 \|z_{0,N}'\|\le CR,\qquad
 \|w_{0,N}'\ell_N\|\le CR^2
\]
and the fast and slow convolution widths \(O(\delta)\) and
\(O(\delta/R)\) give
\begin{equation}
 \|\mathcal K_{Zp}^R\|\le CR^2,\qquad
 \|\mathcal K_{pZ}^R\|\le C,\qquad
 \|\mathcal K_{Zp}^R\mathcal K_{pZ}^R\|
 \le CR^2<\frac12.                               \label{eq:proof-diagonal-principal-loop}
\end{equation}
Here the first edge uses \(p=R(p/R)\), while the second uses the scalar
Green width before dividing the output by \(R\).  Expanding the causal
two-by-two Volterra system and summing
\cref{eq:proof-diagonal-principal-loop} geometrically therefore gives
\[
 \|\mathbb U_{a,0}(s,u)\|
 \le C e^{-cR(s-u)/\delta}.
\]
The current-delay perturbation has norm \(C\varepsilon\).  In a kernel
norm with half the preceding exponent, its Duhamel norm is at most
\[
 \frac{C\varepsilon}{\delta}
   \int_0^\infty e^{-cRv/\delta}\,\dd v
 \le C\frac{\varepsilon}{R}=o(1).
\]
Neumann inversion proves the zeroth-order part of
\cref{eq:diagonal-current-absorbed-evolution}.  The two endpoint equations
for the evolution family prove its \(i+j=1\) cases after multiplication
by \(\delta\).

The structural layer bound and
\(w_{0,N}'+\varepsilon\mathfrak d_N=O(R)\) on this buffer give
\cref{eq:diagonal-delayed-block-size}: the lower component of
\(\mathbb B_k\) is \(O(\varepsilon R)\), which becomes
\(O(\varepsilon)\) after division by \(R\).  Finally, integrate
\cref{eq:diagonal-current-absorbed-evolution} against
\cref{eq:diagonal-delayed-block-size}.  The exponential has width
\(O(\delta/R)\), so the prefactor \(\delta^{-1}\) leaves precisely
\(C\varepsilon/R\).  This proves
\cref{eq:diagonal-one-generation} and the claimed scope of the diagonal
specialization.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of
\cref{prop:attracting-finite-generation-flushing}]
Since \(|q_0|\le q_+\),
\[
 |\bar r_a(s)-r_{\rm out}|\le q_+\delta L_b=o(1)
\]
uniformly for the choice \cref{eq:attracting-flush-buffer-length}.  The same
speed bound gives \(S_a\ge c/\delta\), whereas
\(L_b=O(S_\delta)\).  After decreasing \(\delta_0\), this proves
\(L_b<S_a\) and \cref{eq:attracting-outer-buffer-range}.

We now work in fold time.  Let \(\Theta_+=\{\theta_k:k\in\mathcal I_+\}\)
and fix the finite seam set
\begin{equation*}
 \Sigma_b=\left\{\theta_{i_1}+\cdots+\theta_{i_n}\in[0,L_b]:
        n\ge0,\ \theta_{i_j}\in\Theta_+\right\}.
\end{equation*}
It is finite because
\(n\min\Theta_+\le L_b\).  Let
\(PC_{\Theta_+}^d([0,L_b];E_N\times\R)\) be the Banach space of continuous
functions whose restrictions to the closures of the components of
\([0,L_b]\setminus\Sigma_b\) are \(W^{d,\infty}\), with one-sided traces at
the seams.  On this space use the strong norm
\begin{equation}
 \|x\|_{\mathfrak S_{a,d}}
 =\max_{0\le i\le d}\delta^i
   \operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{0\le s\le L_b}
   \left\{\|\partial_s^iZ(s)\|_N
             +\frac{|\partial_s^ip(s)|}{r_{\rm out}}\right\}.   \label{eq:attracting-piecewise-strong-norm}
\end{equation}
The displayed expression is a complete norm because the partition is
finite.  Translation by any \(\theta_k\in\Theta_+\) maps its seam set into
\(\Sigma_b\), and convolution with the smooth evolution family maps each
cell into the same piecewise Sobolev class.  Thus the operators below act on
this fixed Banach space.  Define
\begin{align}
 (\mathbb K_ax)(s)
 &=
 \delta^{-1}\sum_{k\in\mathcal I_+}
 \int_{\theta_k}^{s}
   \mathbb U_{a,\rm cur}(s,u)\mathbb B_k(u)
                 Z(u-\theta_k)\,\dd u,            \label{eq:attracting-generation-operator}\\
 x_{\rm col}(s)
 &=
 \mathbb U_{a,\rm cur}(s,0)\binom{\gamma(0)}{b_p}\notag\\
 &\quad+
 \delta^{-1}\sum_{k\in\mathcal I_+}
 \int_0^{\min\{s,\theta_k\}}
   \mathbb U_{a,\rm cur}(s,u)\mathbb B_k(u)
                 \gamma(u-\theta_k)\,\dd u.       \label{eq:attracting-direct-collar-term}
\end{align}
An integral whose lower endpoint exceeds its upper endpoint is zero.
Variation of constants gives the exact equation
\begin{equation}
 x=x_{\rm col}+\mathbb K_ax.                      \label{eq:attracting-generation-fixed-point}
\end{equation}

The fixed-support layer-sum bound gives the dimension-uniform estimate
\begin{equation*}
 \sum_{k\in\mathcal I_+}
 \|\mathbb B_k(s)\|_{\mathcal L(\R^N,
          (E_N\times\R,\|\cdot\|_{a,\rm out}))}
 \le C\varepsilon.
\end{equation*}
Differentiating
\cref{eq:attracting-generation-operator} on each cell, and using
\cref{eq:current-absorbed-attracting-evolution}, gives
\begin{equation}
 \|\mathbb K_a\|_{\mathcal L(\mathfrak S_{a,d})}
 \le C_d\varepsilon
 \le C_d\delta^{2-2\vartheta}=:\beta_{a,\delta}.   \label{eq:attracting-generation-number}
\end{equation}
For the old-history term, evaluation behind the seam costs at most
\(e^{2\kappa_{r_{\rm out}}\theta_m}\le\delta^{-2\vartheta}\), but it is
multiplied by \(\varepsilon\).  The endpoint term has no such loss.  Hence
\begin{equation}
 \|x_{\rm col}\|_{\mathfrak S_{a,d}}
 \le C_d\left\{\|\gamma\|_{\mathrm{cap},h,r_{\rm out}}
                 +\frac{|b_p|}{r_{\rm out}}\right\}.           \label{eq:attracting-direct-collar-bound}
\end{equation}
For completeness, the derivative estimate follows cell by cell from
Leibniz' rule.  At differentiation order \(j+1\), an endpoint term contains
\(\delta^{-1}\partial_s^j\{\mathbb B_k(s)Z(s-\theta_k)\}\), hence only
derivatives of \(Z\) through order \(j\).  The factor
\(O(\varepsilon/\delta)\) is absorbed by the next \(\delta\)-weight, while
derivatives of the kernel are controlled by
\cref{eq:current-absorbed-attracting-evolution}.  Induction through order
\(d\) proves the same bound on every component of the fixed partition, with
no dependence on the number of seams.  Neumann inversion of
\cref{eq:attracting-generation-fixed-point} therefore gives the same bound
for \(x\).

Expand the inverse only \(M\) generations:
\begin{equation}
 x=\sum_{n=0}^{M-1}\mathbb K_a^nx_{\rm col}
                  +\mathbb K_a^Mx.                \label{eq:attracting-finite-generation-expansion}
\end{equation}
Consider \(s\in[L_b-\theta_m,L_b]\).  A word with \(n\le M-1\) shifted
edges contains current evolution intervals whose total length is at least
\(s-n\theta_m\) for the endpoint term and at least
\(s-(n+1)\theta_m\) for an old-history insertion.  By
\cref{eq:attracting-flush-buffer-length}, the latter, smaller quantity is
at least \(L_0+1\).  Multiplying the evolution estimates along the word
therefore gives \(e^{-cL_0/\delta}\).  More explicitly, an \(n\)-edge term
is an integral over an ordered simplex in variables \(u_1,\ldots,u_n\);
the shifted constraints are
\(u_{j+1}\le u_j-\theta_{i_j}\).  Summing the lengths of the intervening
current-evolution factors telescopes exactly to
\(s-v-\sum_{j=1}^n\theta_{i_j}\), where \(v=0\) for an endpoint word and
\(0\le v\le\theta_m\) for an old-history insertion.  This gives the two
lower bounds just displayed.
Differentiating the simplex integral creates either kernel derivatives or
boundary faces.  The former are canceled by the strong \(\delta\)-weights,
and a boundary face merely removes one convolution without changing the
telescoping lower bound.  Since \(n\le M-1\) and \(d\) are fixed, the
remaining convolution polynomials are absorbed by decreasing \(c\).
Consequently,
\begin{align}
 \left\|\sum_{n=0}^{M-1}\mathbb K_a^nx_{\rm col}
       \right\|_{\mathfrak S_{a,d};
             [L_b-\theta_m,L_b]}
 &\le C_de^{-cL_0/\delta}\mathcal D_a,\notag\\
 \|\mathbb K_a^Mx\|_{\mathfrak S_{a,d}}
 &\le C_d\delta^{M(2-2\vartheta)}\mathcal D_a,     \label{eq:attracting-word-tail-bounds}\\
 \mathcal D_a
 &=\|\gamma\|_{\mathrm{cap},h,r_{\rm out}}
                 +\frac{|b_p|}{r_{\rm out}}.\notag
\end{align}
Condition \cref{eq:attracting-flush-generation-count} implies \(M>d\).
Here the required smoothing can be seen without invoking an unstated
theorem.  Continuity of \(\gamma\) and \(Z(0)=\gamma(0)\) first gives a
classical \(C^1\) solution for \(s>0\).  Inductively, if the solution is
\(C^j\) after \((j-1)\theta_m\), differentiating the retarded equation on
\([j\theta_m,L_b]\) reads only values from the preceding \(C^j\) region and
gives \(C^{j+1}\) regularity there.  Since
\(L_b-\theta_m>M\theta_m>d\theta_m\), the whole exit interval is genuinely
\(C^d\), even when the initial seam was not compatible through order \(d\).

On the exit interval, \(\partial_s\bar r_a=\delta q_0(\bar r_a)\),
\(|q_0|\asymp1\), and \(|r|\asymp r_{\rm out}\).  Repeated chain rule gives
\begin{equation}
 \max_{0\le j\le d}\sup_{J_b}|r|^j|\partial_r^jf|
 \le C_d\delta^{-2d}
   \max_{0\le i\le d}\delta^i
       \sup_{L_b-\theta_m\le s\le L_b}|\partial_s^if|.         \label{eq:attracting-strong-to-slow}
\end{equation}
The corresponding estimate for \(p\) has the harmless outer factor
\(|r|^{-1}\) already present in the
\(\mathsf P_\varepsilon^d\)-norm.

It remains to recover \(Q\) without losing another derivative.  With
\(H=0\), the scalar row and the algebraic row in
\cref{eq:fixed-phase-normal-block} combine exactly to
\[
 \delta p_s=\varepsilon
       (w_{0,N}'+\varepsilon\mathfrak d_N)Q.
\]
After reparameterization,
\begin{equation}
 Q_b(r)=\frac{q_0(r)p_b'(r)}
              {w_{0,N}'(r)+\varepsilon\mathfrak d_N(r)}.       \label{eq:attracting-Q-from-p}
\end{equation}
The jet bound above and \(|w_{0,N}'|\asymp r_{\rm out}\) give explicitly
\begin{equation*}
 \max_{0\le j\le d-1}
 \sup_{J_b}r_{\rm out}^{j+1}
 \left|\partial_r^j
   \{w_{0,N}'+\varepsilon\mathfrak d_N\}^{-1}\right|\le C_d.
\end{equation*}
Hence the
\(\mathsf P_\varepsilon^d\)-bound for \(p_b\) controls
\(Q_b\) in \(\mathsf Q_\varepsilon^{d-1}\).
Applying \cref{eq:attracting-strong-to-slow} to
\cref{eq:attracting-word-tail-bounds}, and inserting the common
\(\varepsilon^{-1}\) normalization, proves
\cref{eq:attracting-finite-generation-flush}.

Finally, \(S_\delta/\delta\to\infty\), so
\(\delta^{-2d}e^{-cA_dS_\delta/\delta}\) is smaller than every algebraic
power.  Moreover,
\[
 M(2-2\vartheta)-2d
 >2M(1-2\vartheta)-2d>0,
\]
which proves the last assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of
\cref{lem:repelling-component-generations}]
The exact algebra comes first.  From
\cref{eq:current-absorbed-delay-rows},
\[
 b_{k,N}=\varepsilon
    (w_{0,N}'+\varepsilon\mathfrak d_N)c_{k,N}
 =\varepsilon a_pc_{k,N}.
\]
Therefore the coefficient of \(Z\) in the second row of
\(\mathbb A_{\rm cur}\) is
\[
 -w_{0,N}'\ell_N-\varepsilon\mathfrak d_N\ell_N
       +\sum_{k\in\mathcal I_+}b_{k,N}
 =-a_p\left(\ell_N-\varepsilon
                 \sum_{k\in\mathcal I_+}c_{k,N}\right).
\]
The first row is split in the same way.  This proves
\cref{eq:repelling-component-equations}, including all four signs.

We next supply the weighted jets on the entire repelling branch; the
fixed-annulus estimate \cref{eq:current-absorbed-block-jets} alone is not
enough near \(-\rho_\delta\).  Write
\[
 m_N(r)=\frac{w_{0,N}'(r)}r,
\]
using the removable value at \(r=0\).  The critical-curve jets give
\[
 0<c_m\le |m_N(r)|\le C_m,\qquad
 \max_{j\le d+2}\bigl\{|\partial_r^jm_N(r)|
             +|\partial_r^j(m_N^{-1})(r)|\bigr\}\le C_d.
\]
For the shared-resource reduced speed,
\begin{equation}
 q_0(r)=\frac{r-\varepsilon\nu}{w_{0,N}'(r)}
       =\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon\nu}{r}\right)m_N(r)^{-1}.
                                                               \label{eq:repelling-q0-quotient}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for \(1\le n\le d+1\),
\begin{equation}
 |r|^{n-1}|\partial_r^nq_0(r)|
 \le C_d\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{|r|^2}\right)
 \le C_d,                                            \label{eq:repelling-q0-improved-jets}
\end{equation}
because
\(\varepsilon/\rho_\delta^2=4\alpha^2/S_\delta^2\).
The gain of one power of \(|r|\) here is essential; the ordinary weighted
\(q_0\)-bound would not control the jets of \(\mathfrak d_N\).

Put
\[
 f_N(r)=r\mathbf1+z_{0,N}(r),\qquad
 r_k(r)=\Phi_{q_0}^{-\delta\theta_k}(r).
\]
At each retained \(r\), choose a same-sign local annulus on which
\(|u|\asymp |r|\) and which contains the complete \(q_0\)-backtrack tube.
On this annulus
\(\delta\theta_m/|r|\le C/S_\delta\) and
\(\|f_N\|_{d+2,|r|}\le C_d|r|\).  The composition-difference part of
\cref{prop:physical-backtrack-calculus}, applied on that annulus, therefore
gives
\begin{equation}
 |r|^n\left\|
 \partial_r^n\{f_N(r)-f_N(r_k(r))\}\right\|_N
 \le C_d\delta,\qquad 0\le n\le d+1.              \label{eq:repelling-backtrack-difference-jets}
\end{equation}
The common layer-sum bound and the definition of
\(\mathcal D_{0,c,N}\) imply
\begin{equation}
 |r|^n|\partial_r^n\mathcal D_{0,c,N}(r)|
 \le C_d\delta,\qquad 0\le n\le d+1.              \label{eq:repelling-D0-weighted-jets}
\end{equation}
Since \(\mathfrak d_N=(\mathcal D_{0,c,N}-q_0)'\),
\cref{eq:repelling-q0-improved-jets,eq:repelling-D0-weighted-jets}
give, for \(0\le j\le d\),
\[
 |r|^j|\partial_r^j\mathfrak d_N|
 \le C_d\left\{\frac{\delta}{|r|}
          +1+\frac{\varepsilon}{|r|^2}\right\}\le C_d.
\]
This is \cref{eq:repelling-d-weighted-jets}.

The principal coefficient estimates give
\[
 |r|^j\|\partial_r^j(\mathcal C_N-A_N)\|\le C_d|r|,
 \qquad
 |r|^j\bigl\{\|\partial_r^jz_{0,N}'\|
       +\|\partial_r^j\ell_N\|
       +|\partial_r^jw_{0,N}'|\bigr\}\le C_d|r|.
\]
Together with \(\varepsilon\le C|r|\), the coefficient definitions and
Leibniz' rule prove \cref{eq:repelling-weighted-block-jets}.  The sign of
\(w_{0,N}'\) on the negative branch and
\(\varepsilon/|r|\le C\delta/S_\delta=o(1)\) prove both sides of
\cref{eq:repelling-component-sizes}.  Weighted differentiation of
\(a_p^{-1}\), followed by multiplication with the weighted \(q_0\)-jets,
proves \cref{eq:repelling-inverse-coefficient-jets}.

Along \(\bar r_r\), repeated differentiation of
\(\partial_s\bar r_r=\delta q_0(\bar r_r)\) now yields, for
\(0\le m\le d\),
\begin{align}
 &\|\partial_s^m(\mathbb A_Z-A_N)\|
 +|\bar r_r|^{-1}\bigl\{|\partial_s^ma_p|
                  +\|\partial_s^mC_{Zp}\|\bigr\}\notag\\
 &\quad
 +|\bar r_r|^{-2}\|\partial_s^mC_{pZ}\|
 +\varepsilon^{-1}\sum_k\|\partial_s^mB_k^Z\|
 +(\varepsilon|\bar r_r|)^{-1}
       \sum_k\|\partial_s^mB_k^p\|\le C_d.          \label{eq:repelling-fold-time-block-jets}
\end{align}

The transverse diagonal block is a bounded \(O(\varepsilon)\) perturbation
of the uniformly forward-stable \(\mathcal C_N\)-evolution.  Duhamel's
identity, exactly as in the attracting proof, gives the first estimate in
\cref{eq:repelling-component-kernel-bounds}.  The scalar formula in
\cref{eq:repelling-component-kernels} is its exact future-stable kernel.
Differentiating the two endpoint equations for \(U_Z\), and differentiating
the exponential formula for \(u_p\), proves all mixed endpoint derivative
bounds.  The weighted coefficient control in
\cref{eq:repelling-fold-time-block-jets} absorbs the commutators after the
factor \(\delta^{i+j}\) is inserted.  Notice that this construction reverses
only one scalar ODE; it never evolves an RFDE backward.

For the diagonal terminal assertion, take \(d=1\) and suppose
\(R_\delta/2\le|\bar r_r(v)|\le R_\delta\) on \([s,u]\).
Then \(a_p(v)\ge cR_\delta\), and the exact exponential formula gives
\[
 |u_p(s,u)|\le e^{-cR_\delta(u-s)/\delta}.
\]
The two endpoint identities
\[
 \delta\partial_su_p=a_p(s)u_p,\qquad
 \delta\partial_uu_p=-a_p(u)u_p
\]
and \(a_p\le CR_\delta\le C\) prove
\cref{eq:diagonal-repelling-terminal-kernel}.  This also shows directly
why the coefficient of the terminal-fast weight in
\cref{eq:repelling-terminal-edge-weight} cannot be chosen independently
of \(R_\delta\) on terminal buffers containing a fixed positive
fold-time subinterval.

Variation of constants now gives
\cref{eq:repelling-component-LP}.  The sign in its second row is worth
checking: if \(\delta p_s=a_pp+f_p\) and \(p(S_r)=0\), then
\(p=-\mathcal G_p^+f_p\).  Since the current coupling there is
\(-C_{pZ}Z\), it contributes \(+\mathcal K_{pZ}Z\).

We next close the current loop before introducing any delay generation.
In the norm \cref{eq:repelling-component-strong-space}, the source
\(C_{Zp}p\) is \(O(|\bar r_r|^2)\) times the normalized state.  The fast
transverse Green denominator is bounded below independently of \(r\), so
\begin{equation}
 \|\mathcal K_{Zp}\|\le Cr_{\rm out}^2.             \label{eq:repelling-KZp-proof}
\end{equation}
For the reverse edge, put \(R(s)=|\bar r_r(s)|\).  Since
\(R_s=-\delta q_0(\bar r_r)\), \(a_p\asymp R\), and
\(\partial_u\Psi_p(s,u)=a_p(u)/\delta\), one has
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd R(u)^2}{\dd\Psi_p(s,u)}
 =-\frac{2\delta^2q_0(\bar r_r(u))R(u)}{a_p(u)}
 \le C\delta^2.                                     \label{eq:repelling-radius-action-motion}
\end{equation}
Thus, with \(\xi=\Psi_p(s,u)\),
\[
 R(u)\le\{R(s)^2+C\delta^2\xi\}^{1/2}.
\]
Changing variables from \(u\) to \(\xi\), and using
\(\dd u=\delta a_p(u)^{-1}\dd\xi\), gives the explicit bound
\begin{equation}
 \frac1{R(s)}\delta^{-1}
 \int_s^{S_r}e^{-c\Psi_p(s,u)}
       R(u)^2\,\dd u
 \le\frac C{R(s)}
       \int_0^\infty e^{-c\xi}
          \{R(s)^2+C\delta^2\xi\}^{1/2}\,\dd\xi
 \le C\left(1+\frac{\delta}{R(s)}\right)\le C.      \label{eq:repelling-KpZ-integral}
\end{equation}
Here \(\delta/R(s)\le C/S_\delta\).  Endpoint differentiation produces
either another integral with a polynomial factor in \(\xi\), controlled by
the same calculation and
\cref{eq:repelling-fold-time-block-jets}, or a lower-limit boundary term.
Each such boundary term contains
\(C_{pZ}(s)/a_p(s)=O(R(s))\), exactly the factor needed by the scalar
normalization.  Induction through order \(d\) therefore proves
\(\|\mathcal K_{pZ}\|\le C_d\), without an
\(r_{\rm out}/R(s)\) loss.  The fixed decrease of
\(r_{\rm out}\) made for \cref{eq:principal-green-loop} can therefore be
retained so that the product norm is below \(1/4\).  Indeed, for component
data \((d_Z,d_p)\), the current problem is
\[
 Z=d_Z-\mathcal K_{Zp}p,\qquad
 p=d_p+\mathcal K_{pZ}Z,
\]
and hence
\begin{equation}
 Z=\sum_{\ell=0}^\infty
   (-\mathcal K_{Zp}\mathcal K_{pZ})^\ell
       (d_Z-\mathcal K_{Zp}d_p),\qquad
 p=d_p+\mathcal K_{pZ}Z.                           \label{eq:repelling-current-loop-series}
\end{equation}
This also proves uniqueness.

The oriented edge weights are proved before any full mixed kernel is formed.
Choose \(\kappa\) below both constants in
\cref{eq:repelling-component-kernel-bounds}.  Then
\[
 W_Z(s,u)\|U_Z(s,u)\|\le C_de^{-c(s-u)/\delta},
 \qquad
 W_p(s,u)|u_p(s,u)|\le C_de^{-c\Psi_p(s,u)}
\]
after decreasing \(c\).  If \([s,u]\) is contained in an outer interval
where \(a_p\ge c_{\rm out}>0\), then
\(\Psi_p(s,u)\ge c_{\rm out}(u-s)/\delta\), which proves the
\(W_{p,{\rm out}}\) version.  These are two-point kernel estimates; the last
one is deliberately not extended across the inner part of the branch.

Expand \cref{eq:repelling-current-loop-series} and use Fubini on each
finite word.  Multiplication of adjacent edge kernels multiplies their
weights, while the coupling at the common vertex has the sizes in
\cref{eq:repelling-component-sizes}.  Use half of each available kernel
exponent as the bookkeeping weight.  The remaining half gives the same
Green denominators, so the weighted loop is still bounded by
\(Cr_{\rm out}^2\).  The fixed choice of \(r_{\rm out}\) is made small
enough that this is one number \(q_{\rm loop}<1\).  Thus a word with
forward edges \((u_j,s_j)\), future scalar edges \((s'_j,u'_j)\), and
\(\ell\) current loops is bounded, relative to its unweighted coefficient
product, by
\begin{equation}
 C_dq_{\rm loop}^{\ell}
 \exp\left\{-c\sum_j\frac{s_j-u_j}{\delta}
             -c\sum_j\Psi_p(s'_j,u'_j)\right\}.     \label{eq:repelling-decorated-word-action}
\end{equation}
Endpoint differentiation either differentiates one kernel or exposes a
boundary face.  The kernel jets and
\cref{eq:repelling-fold-time-block-jets} give the same estimate for every
such word through order \(d\).  Summing
\(\sum_{\ell\ge0}q_{\rm loop}^{\ell}\) proves precisely the local,
oriented assertion following
\cref{eq:repelling-current-loop-bound}; no global weighted operator claim is
used.

We make the function space explicit.  If
\(\mathcal I_+=\varnothing\), the strict generation operator is zero, the
additive seam set is \(\{0\}\), and every conclusion below follows directly
from the current-loop solution.  Hence assume
\(\mathcal I_+\ne\varnothing\).  The positive delays then have a positive
minimum, so only finitely many of their finite sums lie in \([0,S_r]\).
The corresponding continuous piecewise \(W^{d,\infty}\) space is Banach.
A delay shift moves a seam to another member of the same additive set;
\(\mathcal G_Z\) and \(\mathcal G_p^+\) preserve the partition and create
no reflected seam.  Thus every operator in
\cref{eq:repelling-component-generation-recursion} acts on one fixed
space.  Its bounds do not depend on the number of partition cells.

It remains to estimate a strict generation.  For a current path \(y\), the
strict \(Z\)-source has size \(O(\varepsilon)y\).  The strict scalar source
has the exact factor
\[
 B_k^py=-\varepsilon a_pc_{k,N}y.
\]
The future scalar Green division cancels \(a_p\), leaving
\(O(\varepsilon)y\) in \(p\), or
\(O(\varepsilon/|\bar r_r|)y\) in the normalized state.  Delay translation
changes \(|\bar r_r|\) by a uniformly comparable factor because
\(\delta\theta_m/\rho_\delta=O(S_\delta^{-1})\).  After the current loop is
summed,
\begin{equation}
 \|\mathscr T_ry\|_{\mathfrak S_{r,d}}
 \le C_d\frac{\varepsilon}{\rho_\delta}
          \|y\|_{\mathfrak S_{r,d}},               \label{eq:repelling-strict-generation-proof}
\end{equation}
where \(\mathscr T_r\) denotes one complete recursion step in
\cref{eq:repelling-component-generation-recursion}.  Cellwise
differentiation is triangular, as in the attracting proof, and uses no
derivative beyond order \(d\).

The direct old-history source is read only in the first delay window.  Its
cap loss is at most \(\delta^{-2\vartheta}\), but every such read is
multiplied by \(\varepsilon\); hence it is absorbed in the displayed bound
for \(x^{[0]}\).  The endpoint and terminal lifts have the stated size by
the two diagonal kernels and the current-loop estimate.  This proves the
first line of \cref{eq:repelling-generation-number}.

For the edge count, split linearly
\[
 x^{[n]}=x_E^{[n]}+x_U^{[n]}
       +\sum_{k_0\in\mathcal I_+}x_{H,k_0}^{[n]}.
\]
The endpoint root \(E\) has insertion label \(0\), and the terminal root
\(U\) has insertion label \(S_r\).  A history root \(H,k_0\), at integration
label \(v_0\in[0,\theta_{k_0})\), reads
\(\gamma(\sigma_0)\) at
\(\sigma_0=v_0-\theta_{k_0}\).  Thus it contains the direct collar edge
\(\sigma_0\to v_0=\sigma_0+\theta_{k_0}\).  Applying the recursion \(n\)
times adds exactly \(n\) further \(\mathscr S\)-edges in all three cases.
This proves the edge convention stated in the lemma.

Since
\[
 \frac{\varepsilon}{\rho_\delta}
 =\frac{2\alpha\delta}{S_\delta}
 \le C\frac{\delta^{1-2\vartheta}}{S_\delta},
\]
the second line follows.  The recursion therefore converges absolutely.
Its sum satisfies the exact component equations, and the uniqueness in
\cref{prop:fixed-phase-green-collar-buffer} identifies it with the stated
mixed-boundary solution.  By construction, only a strict shifted
\(Z(s-\theta_k)\) of the current path raises the recursion index; the direct
old-history edge, future scalar propagation, and all current loops do not.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of
\cref{prop:diagonal-terminal-component-green}]
We first verify the window geometry.  The reduced speed is bounded above
and below away from zero, while
\[
 \frac{R_\delta}{\delta S_\delta}
 =\varkappa\delta^{-1/2}S_\delta^{1/2}\longrightarrow\infty .
\]
Thus \(S_\sigma>L_b+\theta_m\) for small \(\delta\).  Along either
shifted path the scalar coordinate moves by at most
\[
 q_+\delta(L_b+\theta_m)\le C_{A,M}\delta S_\delta
 =C_{A,M}\rho_\delta .
\]
The attracting endpoint is \(\rho_\delta\), which proves the first line
of \cref{eq:terminal-component-annuli}.  The repelling endpoint is
\(-R_\delta\), and
\(\rho_\delta/R_\delta\to0\), which proves the second line after one more
decrease of \(\delta_0\).  The fixed annulus ratios also give
\begin{equation}
 |\bar r_{\sigma,T}(t)|
   +\frac{\varepsilon}{|\bar r_{\sigma,T}(t)|^2}
 \asymp m_{\sigma,T}
 \quad(-\theta_m\le t\le L_b).                   \label{eq:proof-terminal-weight-comparison}
\end{equation}

Only zeroth-order coefficient bounds are needed for the first-order
terminal estimate.  We record them explicitly because a second
\(r\)-derivative of \(q_0\) is not uniform at \(r=\rho_\delta\).  Write
\[
 w_{0,N}'(r)=r\,m_N(r).
\]
The critical-curve bounds give common bounds for \(m_N\), its reciprocal,
and their first derivatives.  Moreover,
\[
 q_0(r)=\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon\nu}{r}\right)m_N(r)^{-1}.
\]
On either terminal annulus,
\[
 \frac{\delta}{|r|}\le\frac C{S_\delta},\qquad
 \frac{\varepsilon}{|r|^2}\le\frac C{S_\delta^2}.
                                                        \tag{*}
\]
The order-one instance of the backtrack difference estimate used in
\cref{eq:repelling-backtrack-difference-jets} therefore gives
\(|\mathcal D_{0,c,N}'|\le C_{A,M}\), and the displayed quotient gives
\(|q_0'|\le C_{A,M}\).  Hence
\[
 |\mathfrak d_N|
 =|(\mathcal D_{0,c,N}-q_0)'|\le C_{A,M}.          \label{eq:proof-terminal-d-bound}
\]
No bound for \(q_0''\) is used.

Use the component notation of
\cref{eq:repelling-component-coefficients} on both branches.  If
\[
 a_p=w_{0,N}'+\varepsilon\mathfrak d_N,
\]
then \(-a_p\asymp\rho_\delta\) on the attracting window and
\(a_p\asymp R_\delta\) on the repelling window.  With
\(r_T=r_{\sigma,T}\), the remaining bounds are
\begin{align}
 \|\mathbb A_Z-A_N\|&\le C_{A,M}r_T,&
 \|C_{Zp}\|&\le C_{A,M}r_T,&
 \|C_{pZ}\|&\le C_{A,M}r_T^2,\notag\\
 \sum_{k\in\mathcal I_+}\|B_k^Z\|
 &\le C_{A,M}\varepsilon,&
 \sum_{k\in\mathcal I_+}|B_k^p|
 &\le C_{A,M}\varepsilon r_T .                  \label{eq:proof-terminal-component-ledger}
\end{align}
These follow from
\cref{eq:principal-green-coefficients,%
eq:current-absorbed-delay-blocks,eq:proof-terminal-d-bound}.

We next invert the current, unshifted component problem.  Let \(U_Z\) be
the forward transverse evolution.  On both branches,
\[
 \|U_Z(t,u)\|\le Ce^{-c(t-u)/\delta},\qquad u\le t.
                                                        \label{eq:proof-terminal-fast-kernel}
\]
On the attracting branch the scalar line is forward stable and has kernel
\[
 |u_{p,a}(t,u)|
 \le Ce^{-c\rho_\delta(t-u)/\delta},\qquad u\le t.
                                                        \label{eq:proof-terminal-attracting-scalar}
\]
On the repelling branch it is selected from the future, and
\cref{eq:diagonal-repelling-terminal-kernel} gives
\[
 |u_{p,r}(t,u)|
 \le Ce^{-cR_\delta(u-t)/\delta},\qquad t\le u.
                                                        \label{eq:proof-terminal-repelling-scalar}
\]
Only the last scalar formula is run backward; it is an ordinary
one-dimensional Green integral, not backward RFDE evolution.

Let \(\mathcal K_{Zp}\) and \(\mathcal K_{pZ}\) be the corresponding
component Green maps.  In the norm
\[
 \|(Z,p)\|_{T,0}
 =\sup_{0\le t\le L_b}
       \left\{\|Z(t)\|_N+\frac{|p(t)|}{m_T}\right\},
 \qquad m_T=m_{\sigma,T},
\]
the fast transverse width and the scalar width \(\delta/r_T\) give
\begin{equation}
 \|\mathcal K_{Zp}\|\le C_{A,M}r_Tm_T,\qquad
 \|\mathcal K_{pZ}\|\le C_{A,M}\frac{r_T}{m_T}.
                                                        \label{eq:proof-terminal-current-loop}
\end{equation}
Indeed, \(p=m_T(p/m_T)\), so
\(C_{Zp}p=O(r_Tm_T)(p/m_T)\).  Conversely,
\(C_{pZ}Z=O(r_T^2)Z\); scalar integration divides by \(r_T\), and
normalizing its output by \(m_T\) gives the second bound.  Their product
is at most \(C_{A,M}r_T^2\).  Thus the product in
\cref{eq:proof-terminal-current-loop} is smaller than \(1/4\) for small
\(\delta\).  The same current-loop series as in
\cref{eq:repelling-current-loop-series}, with both component kernels
forward on the attracting branch, therefore has a uniform inverse.
On the repelling branch this inverse is not an evolution family.  Instead,
the series defines the four normalized blocks
\(\mathbb G_{\sigma,T}^{ij}(t,u)\),
\(i,j\in\{Z,p\}\), of a two-sided current Green kernel.  Every term is an
alternating word of forward transverse edges and, on the repelling branch,
future scalar edges.  If their respective lengths are \(f_j\) and \(h_j\),
telescoping the intermediate labels gives
\[
 \sum_j f_j-\sum_j h_j=t-u,
 \qquad
 \sum_j f_j+\sum_j h_j\ge |t-u|.
\]
All transverse rates are at least \(c/\delta\), and all scalar rates are at
least \(cr_T/\delta\).  Using half of the available exponential weight and
summing the current-loop multiplicities geometrically therefore gives, in
the normalized \((Z,p/m_T)\) component norms,
\begin{equation}
 \|\mathbb G_{\sigma,T}^{ij}(t,u)\|_{j\to i}
 \le C_{A,M}
   \exp\left\{-\frac{cr_T|t-u|}{\delta}\right\}   \label{eq:proof-terminal-full-current-kernel}
\end{equation}
for every block.  On the attracting branch this kernel is the ordinary
forward evolution kernel; on the repelling branch it is genuinely
two-sided.  No backward RFDE evolution is used.  The transverse line is
faster; the displayed common rate is the slower safe rate.

We retain the component kernels when estimating distributed sources.
Using only
\cref{eq:proof-terminal-full-current-kernel} for \(F\) would introduce a
false factor \(r_T^{-1}\).  Instead,
\begin{align}
 \|\mathcal G_ZF\|_\infty
 &\le C\|F\|_\infty,\notag\\
 \frac1{m_T}\|\mathcal G_{p,\sigma}H\|_\infty
 &\le\frac C{r_Tm_T}\|H\|_\infty,               \label{eq:proof-terminal-source-components}
\end{align}
where \(\mathcal G_{p,a}\) is forward and
\(\mathcal G_{p,r}\) is the future scalar Green map.  The direct history
reads have sizes \(O(\varepsilon)\gamma\) and
\(O(\varepsilon r_T)\gamma\) in the two equations.  Together with the
finite boundary lifts and the current-loop inverse,
\cref{eq:proof-terminal-source-components} shows that the zero-generation
solution obeys
\begin{equation}
 \|x^{[0]}\|_{T,0}
 \le C_{A,M}\left\{\|\gamma\|_{C^0}
       +\frac{|b_\sigma|}{m_T}
       +\sup\left(\|F\|_N+\frac{|H|}{r_Tm_T}\right)\right\}.
                                                        \label{eq:proof-terminal-zero-generation}
\end{equation}

A strict delayed evaluation acts only on \(Z\).  By
\cref{eq:proof-terminal-component-ledger}, its two source components
satisfy
\[
 \sum_k\left\{\|B_k^Z\zeta_k\|_N
       +\frac{|B_k^p\zeta_k|}{r_Tm_T}\right\}
 \le C_{A,M}\left(\varepsilon+\frac{\varepsilon}{m_T}\right)
       \max_k\|\zeta_k\|_N .
\]
The current-loop inverse preserves this bound.  Thus the complete local
generation maps satisfy
\[
 \|\mathscr T_{a,T}\|\le C_{A,M}\frac{\varepsilon}{m_{a,T}},
 \qquad
 \|\mathscr T_{r,T}\|\le C_{A,M}\frac{\varepsilon}{m_{r,T}}.
                                                        \label{eq:proof-terminal-generation}
\]
This is \cref{eq:terminal-component-generation-numbers}.  Notice that the
second estimate retains the terminal annulus.  Replacing it by the minimum
radius on the whole repelling branch would give only the coarser
\(\varepsilon/\rho_\delta\) bound.

For small \(\delta\), both numbers in
\cref{eq:proof-terminal-generation} are below \(1/2\).  The generation
series
\[
 x=\sum_{n=0}^\infty\mathscr T_{\sigma,T}^nx^{[0]}
\]
therefore converges in the continuous, finite-seam \(C^0\) space and,
by \cref{eq:proof-terminal-zero-generation}, has the required zeroth-order
bound.  The equation itself gives the first-order estimate without
differentiating a Green kernel.  Indeed,
\cref{eq:proof-terminal-component-ledger} gives, cellwise,
\begin{align*}
 \delta\|Z_t\|_N
 &\le C_{A,M}\left\{\|Z\|_N+\frac{|p|}{m_T}
                         +\|F\|_N\right\},\\
 \frac{\delta|p_t|}{m_T}
 &\le C_{A,M}\left\{\|Z\|_N+\frac{|p|}{m_T}
                         +\frac{|H|}{r_Tm_T}\right\}.
\end{align*}
For the first line, the actual normalized coupling has size
\(O(r_Tm_T)|p|/m_T\).  For the second,
\(r_T^2/m_T\le r_T\), while the delayed scalar row has normalized size
\(O(\varepsilon r_T/m_T)\).  This proves
\cref{eq:terminal-component-source-bound}.  Continuity of the retarded
right-hand side across a seam gives the final \(C^1\) assertion whenever
the data have no seam there.

For the flushing estimate set \(F=H=0\).  On the attracting branch the
root data are the left history and the left scalar row.  On the repelling
branch take \(b_r=0\), so only the left history remains.  Expand only
\(M\) strict generations:
\[
 x=\sum_{n=0}^{M-1}\mathscr T_{\sigma,T}^nx^{[0]}
       +\mathscr T_{\sigma,T}^Mx .               \label{eq:proof-terminal-finite-expansion}
\]
For \(n\le M-1\), an endpoint word has \(n\) strict delay edges and a
history word has one additional direct collar edge.  At an output in
\([L_b-\theta_m,L_b]\), these edges consume at most
\((M+1)\theta_m\).  The remaining current Green travel is at least
\(L_0+1\).  More explicitly, if \(v\) is the root insertion label,
\(\vartheta_1,\ldots,\vartheta_k\) are the direct-history and strict-delay
edge lengths, and \(f_j,h_j\) are the forward and future current-edge
lengths, respectively, then
\[
 \sum_jf_j-\sum_jh_j
   =t-v-\sum_{i=1}^k\vartheta_i,
 \qquad
 \sum_jf_j+\sum_jh_j
   \ge\left|t-v-\sum_{i=1}^k\vartheta_i\right|
   \ge L_0+1.
\]
On the attracting branch this is the simplex telescoping used
in \cref{eq:attracting-word-tail-bounds}, now with the local rate
\(\rho_\delta/\delta\).  On the repelling branch the decorated-word
identity following
\cref{eq:repelling-decorated-word-action} gives the same conclusion:
all transverse edges are faster than the \(R_\delta/\delta\) rate and all
future scalar edges lie in the terminal annulus.  Current-loop
multiplicities sum geometrically.  Therefore the finite sum in
\cref{eq:proof-terminal-finite-expansion} is bounded on the final window
by
\[
 C_{A,M}e^{-cr_TL_0/\delta}
 \left\{\|\gamma\|_{C^0}
              +\mathbf1_{\{\sigma=a\}}\frac{|b_a|}{m_{a,T}}\right\}.
\]
The tail is bounded by the \(M\)-th power of
\cref{eq:proof-terminal-generation}.  Boundary-face words created by one
time differentiation have the same root, output, and delay-edge count.
Equivalently, the extra \(\theta_m\) in
\(L_b=(M+2)\theta_m+L_0+1\) lets the differential equation read the same
estimate one delay earlier.  This proves
\cref{eq:terminal-component-rough-flush,%
eq:terminal-component-flush-errors}.
Finally,
\[
 \frac{\rho_\delta L_0}{\delta}
 =\frac{A}{2\alpha}S_\delta^2,\qquad
 \frac{R_\delta L_0}{\delta}
 =A\frac{R_\delta S_\delta}{\delta},
\]
whereas
\begin{align*}
 m_{a,T}
 &=\rho_\delta+\frac{\varepsilon}{\rho_\delta^2}
   =\frac{\delta S_\delta}{2\alpha}
       +\frac{4\alpha^2}{S_\delta^2}
   \asymp S_\delta^{-2},\\
 m_{r,T}
 &=R_\delta+\frac{\varepsilon}{R_\delta^2}
   \asymp R_\delta,
\end{align*}
and hence
\(\varepsilon/m_{a,T}=O(\delta^2S_\delta^2)\) and
\(\varepsilon/m_{r,T}
 =O(\delta^{3/2}S_\delta^{-3/2})\).
Thus both errors tend to zero.

It remains to record the local phase action.  The algebraic row is
\[
 Q=\varepsilon^{-1}(p-\ell_NZ)
       +\mathscr D_{c,N}^{\rm lin}[\gamma,Z].
\]
On the terminal annulus, \(\|\ell_N\|\le C r_T\), every delayed value
is bounded by the same generation estimate, and
\(m_T\ge r_T\) and \(\varepsilon/m_T\to0\).  Hence
\[
 \|\mathscr D_{c,N}^{\rm lin}[\gamma,Z]\|_\infty
 \le C\{\|\gamma\|_{C^0}+\|Z\|_\infty\}
 \le C\varepsilon\mathcal D_{\sigma,T},
 \qquad
 \|Q\|_\infty\le C_{A,M}m_T\mathcal D_{\sigma,T}.
\]
The scalar coordinate length of the terminal buffer is at most
\(C\delta L_b\le C_{A,M}\rho_\delta\), while \(q_0^{-1}\) is uniformly
bounded.  Since \(\dd r=\delta q_0\,\dd t\), the displayed integral is
also the fold-time phase increment \(\delta\int Q/q_0\,\dd t\).
Integration proves the second line of
\cref{eq:terminal-component-local-action}.  The proof used no endpoint
reference, moving evaluation, or backward history evolution, which also
proves the stated scope boundary.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of
\cref{prop:structured-phase-action-green}]
Write
\[
 R(s)=|\bar r_\sigma(s)|,\qquad
 m(s)=m_\delta(\bar r_\sigma(s)),\qquad
 A=\|a\|_{\mathsf A_\phi^1(I_\sigma)}.
\]
For brevity write \(p=p^\sharp\) throughout this proof.
The shifted source profile gives, on every cell of the finite delay
partition,
\begin{equation}
 \|F_\phi^\sharp(s)\|_N\le C\varepsilon A,
 \qquad
 |H_\phi^\sharp(s)|\le C\frac{\varepsilon^2}{R(s)}A.
                                                        \label{eq:phase-action-source-proof}
\end{equation}
Introduce the order-zero weighted norms
\begin{align*}
 \|(Z,p)\|_{\mathfrak X_{\sigma,m}}
 &=\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{s\in I_\sigma}
   \left\{\frac{\|Z(s)\|_N}{\varepsilon}
          +\frac{|p(s)|}{\varepsilon m(s)}\right\},\\
 \|(F,H)\|_{\mathfrak Y_{\sigma,m}}
 &=\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{s\in I_\sigma}
   \left\{\frac{\|F(s)\|_N}{\varepsilon}
          +\frac{|H(s)|}{\varepsilon R(s)m(s)}\right\}.
\end{align*}
Since
\begin{equation}
 R^2m_\delta(R)=R^3+\varepsilon\ge\varepsilon,       \label{eq:phase-action-weight-root}
\end{equation}
\cref{eq:phase-action-source-proof} implies
\begin{equation}
 \|(F_\phi^\sharp,H_\phi^\sharp)\|_{
          \mathfrak Y_{\sigma,m}}\le CA.             \label{eq:phase-action-source-norm}
\end{equation}

Write \(a_p=w_{0,N}'+\varepsilon\mathfrak d_N\) and use the component
coefficients in \cref{eq:repelling-component-coefficients} on either
prescribed path.  The retained coefficient bounds, together with
\(\varepsilon/R\le\varepsilon/\rho_\delta=o(1)\), give
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{sgn}a_p=(-,+)\quad(\sigma=a,r),\qquad
 |a_p|\asymp R,\quad \|C_{Zp}\|\le CR,\quad
 \|C_{pZ}\|\le CR^2,                               \label{eq:phase-action-component-sizes}
\end{equation}
and
\(\sum_k\|B_k^Z\|\le C\varepsilon\),
\(\sum_k\|B_k^p\|\le C\varepsilon R\).
Let \(U_Z\) denote the forward transverse evolution, and put
\begin{align}
 u_{p,a}(s,u)
 &=\exp\left\{\delta^{-1}\int_u^s a_p(v)\,\dd v\right\}
